Emptiness: A feeling of inner emptiness or a state of feeling no emotion.\n  - Emotional Numbness: A state where emotions feel dulled or paralyzed.\n  - Psychomotor Agitation: Purposeless repetitive behaviors such as inability to sit still, pacing, hand-wringing, or picking at skin.\n  - Feelings of Worthlessness: The feeling that one is useless and has no value.\n  - Ruminative Guilt: A thought pattern of obsessively replaying past minor mistakes or flaws and constantly reproaching oneself.\n  - Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt: Feeling excessive responsibility for trivial matters or daily events and believing one deserves punishment.\n  - Delusional Guilt: Guilt reaching a delusional level, disconnected from reality, involving feeling responsible for unrelated unfortunate events.\n  - Recurrent Thoughts of Death: Passive wish to die (e.g., 'I wish I wouldn't wake up') without active intent to kill oneself.\n  - Suicidal Ideation: Recurring thoughts of 'I want to die' or 'I want to kill myself' without a specific plan.\n  - Referential Delusions: The conviction that ordinary events, remarks, or behaviors have specific relevance to oneself.\n  - Grandiose Delusions: The false belief that one possesses special abilities, power, or fame.\n  - Erotomanic Delusions: The false belief that another person is in love with or has affection for them.\n  - Nihilistic Delusions: A destructive or negative conviction that the world is ending or that a major catastrophe will occur.\n  - They didn't start until a few weeks into this dark period.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how long you've been experiencing these voices and feelings of being watched, and have they been constant since they started or have they varied in intensity or frequency?\n\nPatient: I've been dealing with them for about four months, starting shortly after this deep depression took over. They only happen during this dark period, and they aren't always constant; some days the voices and the feeling of being watched are much stronger and more intense than others.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period where the voices or feelings of being watched occurred without the depressive symptoms, or have they always been present together?\n\nPatient: No, they have never happened when my mood was normal. The voices and the feeling of being watched only occur when I am feeling deeply depressed, and they are always tied to this dark period."

The fact that the psychotic symptoms began after the onset of depressive symptoms and have only occurred during the depressive episodes supports the possibility of Major Depressive Disorder with Psychotic Features. However, the duration and characteristics of the symptoms could also align with Schizoaffective Disorder (Depressive Type), especially if the psychotic symptoms are deemed to be prominent and occur for a substantial portion of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness. Depressed Mood: A persistently sunken mood or feeling of depression.\n  - Tearfulness: A state of frequent crying or inability to hold back tears.\n  - Sadness: A state where a sad or gloomy mood is maintained continuously.\n  - Emptiness: A feeling of inner emptiness or a state of feeling no emotion.\n  - Emotional Numbness: A state where emotions feel dulled or paralyzed.\n  - Psychomotor Agitation: Purposeless repetitive behaviors such as inability to sit still, pacing, hand-wringing, or picking at skin.\n  - Psychomotor Retardation (Motor): Visible slowing of general body movements, such as walking speed or picking up objects.\n  - Psychomotor Retardation (Speech): Long response latency or slowed speech when asked a question.\n  - Feelings of Worthlessness: The feeling that one is useless and has no value.\n  - Ruminative Guilt: A thought pattern of obsessively replaying past minor mistakes or flaws and constantly reproaching oneself.\n  - Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt: Feeling excessive responsibility for trivial matters or daily events and believing one deserves punishment.\n  - Delusional Guilt: Guilt reaching a delusional level, disconnected from reality, involving feeling responsible for unrelated unfortunate events.\n  - Recurrent Thoughts of Death: Passive wish to die (e.g., 'I wish I wouldn't wake up') without active intent to kill oneself.\n  - Suicidal Ideation: Recurring thoughts of 'I want to die' or 'I want to kill myself' without a specific plan.\n  - Suicidal Impulse: Strong, sudden urges to jump from a height or run into traffic without a concrete plan.\n  - Specific Plan for Suicide: Conceiving specific scenarios or methods regarding when, where, and how to die.\n  - Suicide Attempt: Actual actions taken to end one's life or preparatory behaviors like hoarding pills or writing a will.\n  Duration: 5 months\n▸ delusions_and_hallucinations\n  - Persecutory Delusions: The false belief that one is being harmed or monitored by other people or organizations.\n  - Referential Delusions: The conviction that ordinary events, remarks, or behaviors have specific relevance to oneself.\n  - Grandiose Delusions: The false belief that one possesses special abilities, power, or fame.\n  - Erotomanic Delusions: The false belief that another person is in love with or has affection for them.\n  - Nihilistic Delusions: A destructive or negative conviction that the world is ending or that a major catastrophe will occur.\n  - Somatic Delusions: The baseless conviction that there is a serious abnormality with one's body or organs.\n  - Jealous Delusions: The conviction that one's spouse or romantic partner is being unfaithful without sufficient evidence.\n  - Auditory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of hearing voices or sounds when there is no actual sound.\n  - Visual Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of seeing people or objects that do not exist.\n  - Tactile Hallucinations: Experiencing the sensation of being touched or movement on the skin when there is no actual stimulus.\n  - Olfactory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of smelling odors that do not exist.\n  - Gustatory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of tasting flavors that are not actually present.\n▸ Critical Diagnostic Criteria\n  - Additional: delusions_and_hallucinations occur exclusively during the major depressive episode\n\nRules:\n- If the patient can specifically answer based on the profile, set matched=true, list related\n  sections/items in matched_sections, and write a short English strategy in answer_strategy\n  on what tone and content the patient should reply with.\n- If the question is not in the profile or can only be inferred, set matched=false and instruct\n  in answer_strategy to respond briefly (e.g., \"I'm not sure\", \"I haven't thought about it\").\n- If patient_turn_index is 1 (patient's first reply), ONLY focus the strategy on the chief complaint\n  (do NOT reveal other symptoms).\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"matched\": true|false, \"matched_sections\": [\"...\"], \"answer_strategy\": \"...\"}\n"

Erotomanic Delusions: The false belief that another person is in love with or has affection for them.\n  - Nihilistic Delusions: A destructive or negative conviction that the world is ending or that a major catastrophe will occur.\n  - Somatic Delusions: The baseless conviction that there is a serious abnormality with one's body or organs.\n  - Jealous Delusions: The conviction that one's spouse or romantic partner is being unfaithful without sufficient evidence.\n  - Auditory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of hearing voices or sounds when there is no actual sound.\n  - Visual Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of seeing people or objects that do not exist.\n  - Tactile Hallucinations: Experiencing the sensation of being touched or movement on the skin when there is no actual stimulus.\n  - Olfactory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of smelling odors that do not exist.\n  - Gustatory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of tasting flavors that are not actually present.\n▸ Critical Diagnostic Criteria\n  - Additional: delusions_and_hallucinations occur exclusively during the major depressive episode\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: Depressed_Mood\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. Do not list your symptoms unprompted.\n- Speak naturally and briefly, as a real patient would (2–3 sentences max).\n- Do not mention symptoms that the doctor has not asked about.\n- If asked about a symptom not in your profile, say you are not sure.\n- Respond in English.\n\n\n[This Turn: Profile Alignment Result — Follow Strictly]\n(Matched with profile)\nRelated sections: depressive_episode, Critical Diagnostic Criteria\nAnswer strategy: Confirm that both the severe depression and the unusual experiences (like hearing things or having false beliefs) have been ongoing continuously for about five months, and that these experiences have occurred during this depressive period without any real improvement in your mood."
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I've been feeling extremely depressed and sad, and it just feels like I'm stuck in a dark place. This has been going on continuously for about five months now. That's why I finally decided to come to the clinic today.\n\nDoctor: During these five months, have you experienced any periods where your mood significantly improved, or where you felt abnormally happy, irritable, or energetic?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any periods like that at all. My mood has just been consistently low and heavy, and I haven't felt energetic or happy even for a single day.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any unusual perceptions, such as hearing voices or seeing things that others don't, or had any thoughts that seemed unreal or detached from reality during this time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I actually have. Lately, I've been hearing faint whispering and voices when I'm completely alone in the room. I also constantly feel like people on the street are watching me and talking about me, which only started since I've been feeling this depressed.\n\nDoctor: Did the voices, whispering, or feelings of being watched start at the same time as your depressive symptoms, or did they begin before or after your depression started?\n\nPatient: The deep sadness and depression definitely started first, about five months ago. The voices and the feeling of being watched only occur when I am feeling deeply depressed, and they are always tied to this dark period.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks where you experienced the voices or feelings of being watched without any significant depressive symptoms, or have the psychotic symptoms always been accompanied by depression?\n\nPatient: No, I have never experienced those things without the depression. They only ever happen when I am feeling deeply depressed. I've never had a single day where I heard voices or felt watched when my mood was normal.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm that your current episode of depression, including the psychotic symptoms, has lasted continuously for about five months without any significant improvement in your mood?"

The absence of any reported manic episodes, the continuous nature of the depressive symptoms without significant improvement, and the tie between the psychotic symptoms and the depressive state support this diagnosis. Other options like Schizoaffective Disorder are less likely because the patient reports the psychotic symptoms only occur during the depressive episodes and not independently. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol). Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression. Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.
